# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-11-06

## Time Series

| **[Thermally Conditioned Time-Series Forecasting of Grain Growth in Polycrystalline Materials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06843v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Grain growth during heat treatment strongly affects the microstructure of polycrystalline materials and, consequently, their mechanical properties. Although numerical methods such as front-tracking or front-capturing simulations can accurately describe grain growth, their high computational cost limits their use for long or non-isothermal thermal cycles. In previous work, grain growth was successfully predicted under isothermal conditions by treating grain size distributions as time series and forecasting their evolution using recurrent neural networks, allowing complete grain growth trajectories to be generated in seconds rather than through costly PDE-based simulations. In the present work, this forecasting framework is reformulated to handle non-isothermal conditions by explicitly incorporating thermal information into the model architecture. Grain size distributions are encoded using a convolutional neural network, thermal descriptors are embedded through a dedicated multilayer perceptron, and temporal evolution is predicted using a recurrent network. Instantaneous temperature and thermal rate are provided at each time step, allowing the model to adapt its predictions to changing thermal conditions. The framework is trained and evaluated on datasets generated from validated front-tracking simulations across a wide range of non-isothermal thermal paths. The results show that the thermally conditioned model accurately reproduces grain growth dynamics under complex thermal histories, achieving, in k-fold cross-validation over all simulations, mean relative errors of 2.99% (one-hour cycles) and 4.51% (four-hour cycles) with coefficients of determination exceeding 0.99, and mean relative errors between 1.91% and 14.66% on ten simulations following four unseen thermal histories of increasing complexity.</p></details> |  |
| **[Consistent Geometric Deep Learning via Hilbert Bundles and Cellular Sheaves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06395v3)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern deep learning architectures increasingly contend with sophisticated signals that are natively infinite-dimensional, such as time series, probability distributions, or operators, and are defined over irregular domains. Yet, a unified learning theory for these settings has been lacking. To start addressing this gap, we introduce a novel convolutional learning framework for possibly infinite-dimensional signals supported on a manifold. Namely, we use the connection Laplacian associated with a Hilbert bundle as a convolutional operator, and we derive filters and neural networks, dubbed as \textit{HilbNets}. We make HilbNets and, more generally, the convolution operation, implementable via a two-stage sampling procedure. First, we show that sampling the manifold induces a Hilbert Cellular Sheaf, a generalized graph structure with Hilbert feature spaces and edge-wise coupling rules, and we prove that its sheaf Laplacian converges in probability to the underlying connection Laplacian as the sampling density increases. Notably, this result is a generalization to the infinite-dimensional bundle setting of the Belkin \& Niyogi \cite{BELKIN20081289} convergence result for the graph Laplacian to the manifold Laplacian, a theoretical cornerstone of geometric learning methods. Second, we discretize the signals and prove that the discretized (implementable) HilbNets converge to the underlying continuous architectures and are transferable across different samplings of the same bundle, providing consistency for learning. Finally, we validate our framework on synthetic and real-world tasks. Overall, our results broaden the scope of geometric learning as a whole by lifting classical Laplacian-based frameworks to settings where the signal at each point lives in its own Hilbert space.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 Spotlight. 52 pages, 3 figures, 5 tables</p></details> |
| **[From Reconstruction to Imputation: Test-Time Optimization for Missing Time-Series Channels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06612v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Complete sensor-channel failures create a difficult form of time-series missingness: local interpolation is unavailable, and deploying a separately trained imputer for every failure pattern may be impractical. We ask whether a sequence reconstructor trained without artificial mask augmentation and then frozen can be converted into an imputer at inference time. Our method treats the missing entries of each test window as optimization variables, clamps all observations, and backpropagates through the frozen model to minimize reconstruction error only on observed coordinates. The resulting observed-self-consistency objective requires neither artificial mask augmentation nor full-channel dropout during reconstructor training. We characterize when this refinement is informative through the cross-Jacobian from missing inputs to observed residuals, and use an optional temporal-smoothness prior when that coupling is weak. Across five real-world datasets with exactly one channel synthetically masked per test window and four reconstruction architectures, no-prior test-time refinement reduces MAE relative to one-pass reconstruction in 19 of 20 dataset-backbone combinations. Compared with mask-aware imputers trained under either standard structured masks or matched full-channel dropout, the best refined reconstructor achieves the lowest MAE on two datasets and trails the strongest baseline on three. These gains require substantially more inference time and are sensitive to cross-channel coupling. Our results establish test-time self-consistency as a conditional inference-time adapter for frozen reconstruction models that complements mask-aware imputation without retraining the reconstructor.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Fixed Defaults to Calibrated Activation Policies for Long-Term Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06559v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-term time-series forecasting supports decisions in energy, transportation, and other applications where reliable predictions are needed beyond the observed data. Although forecasting research has advanced model architectures, fixed activation configurations provide limited guidance on how activation choices should vary across datasets and prediction horizons. This study aims to establish when task-specific activation choices improve forecasting accuracy and how previous experiments can guide the selection of suitable configurations for new tasks. We develop a calibrated selection policy that learns a reliable default from prior experiments, adapts the activation function and its location to each task, and retains the default when evidence for a change is insufficient. In an evaluation supported by 1,225 Informer training runs across 17 dataset variants and 51 forecasting tasks, the policy reduces mean squared error by 7.86% on average relative to the conventional fixed-activation baseline and improves 72.55% of tasks. These findings establish activation choice and placement as practical opportunities for improving existing forecasting models and show how accumulated experimental evidence can guide configuration decisions without repeating an exhaustive search for every task.</p></details> | <details><summary>36 pa...</summary><p>36 pages, 2 figures. Shijie Xu and Boyan Tang contributed equally. Code: https://github.com/Tt200411/From-Fixed-Defaults-to-Calibrated-Activation-Policies-for-Long-Term-Time-Series-Forecasting</p></details> |
| **[AdaMamba: Adaptive Frequency-Gated Mamba for Long-Term Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23239v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time-series forecasting is foundational to a wide spectrum of web-scale services, spanning personalized recommendations, energy scheduling, and infrastructure capacity planning. Accurate long-term time series forecasting (LTSF) requires models to capture long-range temporal dependencies while adapting to periodic patterns that vary across variables. Although frequency-aware methods offer a global view of temporal dynamics, they typically rely on inflexible heuristic designs, such as predefined period lengths, manually selected or randomly sampled top-k frequency components, and separate spectral transformations. Consequently, they fail to account for time-frequency heterogeneity, where variables that appear synchronized in the time domain can differ substantially in the frequency domain. Meanwhile, Mamba-based forecasters, despite their efficiency in long-range modeling, introduce frequency information only as auxiliary signals, leaving spectral evidence disconnected from state transitions. In this work, we propose AdaMamba, a novel approach that explicitly internalizes input-dependent frequency information into the modeling of temporal dependencies. AdaMamba comprises an interaction learning module and an adaptive frequency-gated state space module: the former captures cross-variable dependencies and multi-scale temporal context, while the latter generates context-dependent frequency bases and unifies temporal forgetting into a single time-frequency gate that directly modulates state evolution. Extensive experiments on seven public LTSF benchmarks and four domain-specific datasets demonstrate that AdaMamba consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines while maintaining competitive computational efficiency.</p></details> |  |
| **[Graph Foundation Models Can Become Effective Multivariate Time Series Anomaly Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06441v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multivariate time series anomaly detection (MTSAD) relies on modeling both temporal dynamics and inter-sensor dependencies to identify complex system faults. Although graph-based detectors explicitly model sensor topologies, they train task-specific networks from scratch on limited target-domain data, remaining vulnerable to spurious correlations and unable to reuse transferable priors. While pretrained Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) offer transferable topological priors and anomaly discriminability, directly adapting them to MTSAD is hindered by cross-modal feature-topology mismatch, unreliable pseudo-anomaly supervision, and decision distortion during node-to-window aggregation. To fill these gaps, we propose a novel adaptation framework, Gfm4Mtsad, which unleashes pretrained GFMs for MTSAD. We introduce the Structure-and-Information Contract Alignment (SICA) module to bridge continuous time series and the pretrained GFM space. This module harmonizes principal subspace token projection, normal-prior-guided graph construction, and residual recovery to suppress spurious edges while retaining fine-grained temporal details. We propose a Mechanism-Aligned Pseudo-Anomaly Screening strategy (MAPS) to steer unsupervised learning. It applies localized dependency breaks while preserving empirical residuals, selecting hard negatives near the normal boundary for targeted supervision. We develop a Tail-Gated Evidence Calibration mechanism (TGEC) to eliminate node-to-window aggregation distortion. It calibrates multi-granular graph responses against normal operational references, strictly preserving benign score rankings while selectively refining extreme anomaly candidates in the tail distribution for reliable window detection and interpretable sensor evidence. Extensive evaluations with two GFM backbones demonstrate that Gfm4Mtsad achieves superior detection performance over existing baselines.</p></details> |  |
| **[Flow Matching with In-Context Priors for Out-of-Distribution Brain Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11833v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Flow matching and diffusion models enable conditional generation across domains ranging from images to proteins, with recent extensions to out-of-distribution contexts. Yet generative models of neural time series have largely remained restricted to categorical conditioning, precluding compositional and zero-shot generalization. In this work, we propose PINC-Flow, a per-timestep conditioned diffusion transformer for generating realistic fMRI brain dynamics during unseen cognitive tasks by injecting both compositional language and optional spatial priors in-context. Such zero-shot generation could enable counterfactual neuroscience by supporting in-silico design and evaluation of novel cognitive experiments before empirical validation. Leveraging this model, we evaluate across hundreds of held-out task conditions and characterize predictive performance in relation to the training manifold. From language alone, the model recovers region-specific recruitment across tasks and held-out spatial activation patterns. Spatial priors, when available, complement the text pathway by anchoring generation in regions of task space where language alone degrades, while retaining the compositional structure needed for counterfactual task specification. To our knowledge this is the first generative model of whole-cortex fMRI dynamics for unseen cognitive tasks, advancing counterfactual neuroscience and data-driven experimental design.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026. Code and pretrained models available at https://github.com/SamGijsen/pinc-flows</p></details> |
| **[Sparse Federated Echo State Networks for Time-Series Classification via Smooth-L1 Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21742v3)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Echo State Networks (ESNs), as lightweight implementations of reservoir computing, provide efficient solutions for temporal data processing in resource-constrained environments. While standard ESNs offer computational efficiency, their dense readout layers inflate the stored model and the per-sample inference cost. This paper introduces a Smooth-L1 regularized ESN (SL1ESN) that promotes sparsity in the readout layer while preserving differentiability for gradient-based optimization. The method employs a twice-differentiable Smooth-L1 approximation compatible with Newton's method, which we exploit both for centralized training and for a second-order (Newton-step) federated aggregation scheme. Under a single fixed shrinkage threshold shared by every dataset and both settings, experiments on seven time-series classification benchmarks show that SL1ESN learns readouts that are 77-95% sparse in the centralized setting and 75-95% sparse in the federated one. On five of the seven benchmarks, the mean accuracy of the sparse readout over ten seeds differs from that of the stronger dense baseline by at most 0.7 points in the centralized setting and 1.6 points in the federated one, differences of the same order as the seed-to-seed variation, while on the remaining two it trails by 2.4-5.8 points. The second-order federated update converges in only a few rounds. The resulting sparsity compresses the stored model, the per-sample inference cost, and the server-to-client broadcast by a comparable factor. These results demonstrate the practical value of sparse readouts for edge computing and IoT deployments.</p></details> | <details><summary>30 pa...</summary><p>30 pages, 8 figures, 7 tables. Extended version of a paper presented at IDEAL 2025. Code: https://github.com/oroboro235/FedESN-SmoothL1. The abstract and the corresponding results and discussion sentences now state that the accuracy margins are differences of ten-seed means, not bounds that hold for other seeds</p></details> |
| **[Does Explanation Correctness Matter? Linking Computational XAI Evaluation to Human Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25251v3)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Explainable AI (XAI) methods are commonly evaluated using functional correctness metrics, also called faithfulness or fidelity, which estimate how closely an explanation reflects the model's reasoning. Higher correctness is assumed to produce better human understanding, but this link has not been tested with controlled levels. We conducted a user study (N=200) that manipulated explanation correctness at four levels (100%, 85%, 70%, 55%) in a synthetic time series classification task where participants could not rely on domain knowledge or visual intuition. Explanations were constructed to resemble feature attributions, with correctness defined against a known ground truth rather than estimated from a trained model. Participants predicted a simulated AI's decisions from these explanations (forward simulation), and we used their forward simulation accuracy as a proxy for understanding. Correctness affected forward simulation accuracy, but not at every level: accuracy dropped at 70% and 55% relative to 100%, with no further reduction below 70% and no conclusive difference between 85% and 100%. Lower correctness reduced how many participants predicted accurately rather than lowering accuracy uniformly, and even with fully correct explanations, only a subset of participants predicted well above chance. In exploratory analyses, some participants predicted decisions accurately but described the wrong pattern when asked what the AI used, and self-reported ratings correlated with forward simulation accuracy only when explanations were fully correct. These findings suggest that, for feature-attribution-style explanations, not all differences in functional correctness translate to differences in human understanding, highlighting the need to validate functional metrics against human outcomes.</p></details> | <details><summary>33 pa...</summary><p>33 pages, 11 figures, 3 tables</p></details> |
| **[fev-bench: A Realistic Benchmark for Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.26468v5)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Benchmark quality is critical for meaningful evaluation and sustained progress in time series forecasting, particularly with the rise of pretrained models. Existing benchmarks often have limited domain coverage or overlook real-world settings such as tasks with covariates. Their aggregation procedures frequently lack statistical rigor, making it unclear whether observed performance differences reflect true improvements or random variation. Many benchmarks lack consistent evaluation infrastructure or are too rigid for integration into existing pipelines. To address these gaps, we propose fev-bench, a benchmark of 100 forecasting tasks across seven domains, including 46 with covariates. Supporting the benchmark, we introduce fev, a lightweight Python library for forecasting evaluation emphasizing reproducibility and integration with existing workflows. Using fev, fev-bench employs principled aggregation with bootstrapped confidence intervals to report performance along two dimensions: win rates and skill scores. We report results on fev-bench for pretrained, statistical, and baseline models and identify promising future research directions.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publu...</summary><p>Publushed at NeurIPS 2026, Evaluations & Datasets Track</p></details> |
| **[Learning From Static Context in Federated Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06064v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time series forecasting is shifting from locally trained, per-series models to global models trained across many heterogeneous series, which requires learning what sets the series apart. When the raw data cannot be centralized, such a global model is trained with federated learning (FL), one client per data owner. State-of-the-art forecasters such as the Temporal Fusion Transformer (TFT) tell series apart through a static input that describes each series and does not change over time. In the special case of a federated setting where each client holds a single series, this static input is constant within each client and varies only across clients. We ask whether a federated TFT can learn from it. Per-batch FedAdam, which aggregates gradients after every batch, uses the static input almost as well as central training, whereas communication-efficient schemes with local epochs (FedAvg, FedAdam+Prox) extract far less from it and gain nothing significant on either application dataset. At the same local computation, an Adam-compatible SCAFFOLD keeps the static pathway responsive on wind power and gains significantly there. On building energy its error still drops, but the static pathway falls silent, so drift and communication frequency cannot be fully separated as the cause. Only per-batch FedAdam matches or beats the per-client local models across all datasets. We confirm the effect on building-energy meter data, a wind-power dataset, and a controlled synthetic dataset. The loss is not specific to the TFT: on the synthetic and the wind data two further architectures with different static-conditioning mechanisms show it as well.</p></details> | <details><summary>4th I...</summary><p>4th IEEE International Conference on Federated Learning Technologies and Applications (FLTA 2026)</p></details> |
| **[Impact of Negative Weights on Performance in Reservoir Computing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06051v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reservoir computing provides an efficient framework for time-series prediction because only the readout layer is trained while the recurrent reservoir remains fixed. In this study, we examine how the negative sign ratio of a signed Erdos-Renyi reservoir affects the internal dynamics and forecasting performance of an echo state network on the Mackey-Glass benchmark. To characterize the reservoir responses, we use two Shannon-entropy-based observables, namely node-state entropy and node-frequency entropy, as empirical proxies for the diversity of reservoir states and temporal activation patterns. We find that intermediate negative sign ratios are associated with richer internal dynamics and substantially lower prediction error than the sign-homogeneous extremes for the task considered here. These results suggest that link-sign composition can be a useful structural parameter in reservoir design for chaotic forecasting. The present findings should be interpreted within the scope of the current benchmark and reservoir setting, and broader validation will be required to establish their generality across other tasks, topologies, and controlled spectral conditions.</p></details> |  |
| **[Time-Varying Tails: Estimation, Inference, and Risk Measurement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06005v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Motivated by evidence that the tails of economic and financial time series need not be stable over time, we develop a framework for studying time-varying tails. We model the extreme value index as a smooth function of time in the locally stationary framework of Zhou and Wu (2009). We estimate it by a ratio-form kernel Hill estimator in which a single intermediate quantile thresholds the whole local window. We establish consistency and asymptotic normality, and we construct uniform confi- dence bands. We also propose a consistent test of tail stability under extremal clustering. Combined with the Weissman extrapolation method, the estimator of the extreme value index yields time- varying extreme Value-at-Risk and Expected Shortfall. In an empirical application, we find the tail of daily loss of S&P 500 is compatible with a constant shape, whereas that of volatility-filtered loss varies significantly.</p></details> |  |
| **[Decouple to Reuse: Fine-Grained Knowledge Augmentation for Time Series Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05993v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time series forecasting is critical for demand prediction and resource management in modern Web services, yet applying pretrained Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) to downstream tasks remains challenging due to domain shifts. Existing retrieval-augmented approaches typically retrieve coarse-grained window contexts and may introduce context-specific noise. To address this problem, we argue that temporal patterns should be reused at a decomposition level, as effective trend and seasonal dynamics may originate from different contexts. We propose \method, a two-stage \underline{F}ine-grained \underline{K}nowledge \underline{A}ugmentation framework that decouples and consolidates pre-training knowledge into compact, task-shared residual codebooks to enhance frozen TSFMs. Specifically, Stage I constructs dual-branch residual codebooks for decoupled trend and seasonal signals, while Stage II dynamically retrieves and merges these fine-grained patterns into target representations via task-adaptive aggregation and gate fusion. Extensive experiments across eight real-world benchmarks with four representative TSFM backbones demonstrate the superior effectiveness and computational efficiency of \method, while further validating the strong transferability of the residual codebooks across different backbones. The source code is available at the anonymous repository: https://github.com/yuppielqx/TSFM-Component-Token-Adapter</p></details> | 13 pages, 10 figures |
| **[A Granular Framework for Construction Material Price Forecasting: Econometric and Machine-Learning Approaches](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.09360v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The persistent volatility of construction material prices poses significant risks to cost estimation, budgeting, and project delivery, underscoring the urgent need for granular and scalable forecasting methods. This study develops a forecasting framework that leverages the Construction Specifications Institute (CSI) MasterFormat as the target data structure, enabling predictions at the six-digit Section level and supporting detailed cost projections across a wide spectrum of building materials. To enhance predictive accuracy, the framework integrates explanatory variables such as raw material prices, commodity indexes, and macroeconomic indicators. Five time-series models, long short-term memory (LSTM), autoregressive integrated moving average (ARIMA), vector error correction model (VECM), vanilla Transformer, and Chronos-Bolt, were evaluated under both baseline configurations (using CSI data only) and extended versions with explanatory variables. Results demonstrate that incorporating explanatory variables significantly improves predictive performance across all models. Among the tested approaches, the LSTM model consistently achieved the highest accuracy, with root mean squared error (RMSE) values as low as 4.19 and mean absolute percentage error (MAPE) values of 1.60 based on Division 06 as a representative demonstration, representing improvements of up to 59% over the traditional statistical time-series model, ARIMA. Validation across multiple CSI Divisions within the residential-material confirmed the framework's scalability, while Division 06 (Wood, Plastics, and Composites) is presented in detail as a demonstration case. This research offers a robust methodology that enables owners and contractors to improve budgeting practices and achieve more reliable cost estimation at the definitive level.</p></details> |  |
| **[Local Whittle estimation for distributional time series: A regularization framework in Bayes Hilbert space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05765v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study local Whittle estimation of long memory in density-valued time series in Bayes Hilbert space. The centered log-ratio (clr) transformation maps densities to a linear Hilbert space, allowing analysis using standard Hilbert space methods. When densities are reconstructed from finite samples, however, small absolute estimation errors can remain large relative to the true density in low-density tails. Taking logarithms can amplify this noise and introduce systematic distortions that affect memory estimation. We develop a framework for regularizing estimated densities before transformation to improve finite-sample performance while preserving the latent memory target. Using the clr representation, we apply local Whittle estimation to principal component scores along an estimated leading direction of the long-run covariance operator. The framework is based on local Whittle admissibility, defined by uniform asymptotic equivalence between the feasible and latent-score criteria. We separate density-estimation error from the population perturbation induced by regularization and give sufficient admissibility conditions based on density-estimation rates and the temporal dependence of the error components. Under suitable regularity assumptions, these conditions yield consistency for the latent memory parameter. We study additional tail smoothing, a vanishing density floor, and their combination. Monte Carlo experiments show that these procedures can reduce finite-sample bias relative to unregularized kernel density reconstruction. An application to daily distributions of intraday EUR/USD exchange rate returns illustrates the methodology.</p></details> |  |
| **[Sensor Response-Time Reduction using Long-Short Term Memory Network Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.17144v3)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The response time of a biosensor is a crucial metric in safety-critical applications such as medical diagnostics where an earlier diagnosis can markedly improve patient outcomes. However, the speed at which a biosensor reaches a final equilibrium state can be limited by poor mass transport and long molecular diffusion times that increase the time it takes target molecules to reach the active sensing region of a biosensor. While optimization of system and sensor design can promote molecules reaching the sensing element faster, a simpler and complementary approach for response time reduction that is widely applicable across all sensor platforms is to use time-series forecasting to predict the ultimate steady-state sensor response. In this work, we show that ensembles of long short-term memory (LSTM) networks can accurately predict equilibrium biosensor response from a small quantity of initial time-dependent biosensor measurements, allowing for significant reduction in response time by a mean and median factor of improvement of 18.6 and 5.1 respectively. The ensemble of models simultaneously estimates uncertainty, which is vital for ensuring confidence in the predictions and subsequent safety-related decisions that are made. This approach is demonstrated on real-time experimental data collected by exposing porous silicon biosensors to buffered protein solutions using a multi-channel fluidic cell that enables the automated measurement of 100 porous silicon biosensors in parallel. The dramatic improvement in sensor response time achieved using LSTM network ensembles and associated uncertainty quantification opens the door to trustworthy and faster responding biosensors, enabling more rapid medical diagnostics for faster clinical decision making that can lead to improved patient outcomes and healthcare access, as well as quicker identification of toxins in food and the environment.</p></details> | 12 pages, 6 figures |
| **[A Lightweight Multi-View Approach to Short-Term Load Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09220v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time series forecasting is a critical task across domains such as energy, finance, and meteorology, where accurate predictions enable informed decision-making. While transformer-based and large-parameter models have recently achieved state-of-the-art results, their complexity can lead to overfitting and unstable forecasts, especially when older data points become less relevant. In this paper, we propose a lightweight multi-view approach to short-term load forecasting that leverages single-value embeddings and a scaled time-range input to capture temporally relevant features efficiently. We introduce an embedding dropout mechanism to prevent over-reliance on specific features and enhance interpretability. Our method achieves competitive performance with significantly fewer parameters, demonstrating robustness across multiple datasets, including scenarios with noisy or sparse data, and provides insights into the contributions of individual features to the forecast.</p></details> |  |
| **[Bayesian Nonparametric Global-local Clustering of High-dimensional Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.09792v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>There is a rich literature on clustering functional data with applications to time-series modeling, trajectory data, and even spatiotemporal applications. Existing methods are classified into two categories of global clustering that assigns an identical membership over the whole domain, and local clustering that allows multiple memberships based on the sub-domains. Classical literature has typically focused on global clustering that may struggle with degenerate clustering or produce numerous spurious clusters for high-dimensional functions. In contrast, a limited number of local clustering approaches have been proposed that are not scalable to high-dimensional functions and have limited theoretical guarantees. Focusing on basis expansions for high-dimensional functions, we propose a novel global-local framework that partitions coefficients into exclusive subsets and imposes independent Dirichlet process priors on each subset. The proposed framework reduces the total number of possible clustering configurations compared to local clustering and therefore provides greater scalability, while simultaneously providing enhanced flexibility compared to global clustering by relaxing the assumption of identical clustering for all elements in the high-dimensional function. Moreover, the proposed method incorporates spatially correlated errors to provide better model fitting and shows posterior consistency properties that asymptotically recover the true density of random functions. An efficient Markov chain Monte Carlo algorithm is developed for implementation. Extensive simulations involving images illustrate the improved scalability, clustering, and function estimation under the proposed method compared to classical approaches. We apply the proposed approach to a spatial transcriptomics application where the goal is to infer clusters of genes with distinct spatial patterns of expressions.</p></details> |  |
| **[TSFM Meets LLM: Context as Covariates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05515v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Textual information is ubiquitous in real-world time series datasets, appearing as news, reports, and annotations that hold key predictive signals not deducible from numerical history alone. Large Language Models (LLMs) interpret text but lack numerical precision, while Time Series Foundation Models (TSFMs) are quantitatively accurate but cannot reason about the effects of text. Moreover, the lack of large-scale paired multimodal datasets limits progress in bridging this gap. In this work, we address this challenge by providing a scalable approach for generating realistic multimodal datasets by annotating real-world time series with LLMs. Building on this, we propose Context as Covariates (CoCo), a forecasting framework in which an LLM distills its textual reasoning into numerically grounded forecast and confidence covariates that guide a TSFM backbone, thereby exploiting the strengths of both LLMs and TSFMs. We align the two models in stages, first fine-tuning the LLM via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with a reward tied to covariate-induced improvement in forecasting performance, and then fine-tuning the TSFM on covariates generated by the aligned LLM. Our experiments show that models trained on our synthetic multimodal corpus generalize to real benchmarks spanning finance, economics, weather, traffic, and security. Notably, CoCo with Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 as the LLM and Chronos2 as the TSFM achieves up to 12% MAE improvement over state-of-the-art TSFMs and LLM forecasters, such as GPT-4o. Code, checkpoints, and the evaluation datasets are publicly available.</p></details> |  |
| **[CATF: A Manager-Worker Framework for Context-Aware Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05325v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study context-aware time-series forecasting, in which a single target variable is predicted from its own history and a set of contextual variables, such as weather and calendar information. As the context becomes more diverse, a single model struggles to capture all of its regimes, while ensemble learning and Mixture-of-Experts approaches rely on manual data partitioning or soft routing, which increases the inference cost and hinders interpretability. We propose CATF, a Context-Aware Time-Series Forecasting framework built on a Manager-Worker architecture: a Transformer-based Manager routes each input to a single Worker based on the context, using hard routing during both training and inference. The Workers thus specialize without manual data partitioning, and any single-model forecaster can serve as a Worker. On five context-rich datasets, four horizons, and two normalization schemes, CATF improves over its standalone counterpart in 162 of 200 comparisons, the best CATF-enhanced model outperforms the best standalone baseline in 19 of 20 settings, and it outperforms the zero-shot Time-MoE foundation model in 15 of 20 settings. A case study on bike-sharing data shows that the Manager routes inputs by the calendar and that each Worker specializes in a different weekly pattern.</p></details> | <details><summary>21 pa...</summary><p>21 pages of main text (including references), with 4 figures and 3 tables, plus 16 pages of appendices</p></details> |
| **[Parallel Sparse Identification of Nonlinear Dynamics in Python: Task- and Data-Parallel SINDy with Numba, OpenMP, Threads and Multiprocessing for the Lorenz and Rössler Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05215v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The sparse identification of nonlinear dynamics (SINDy) recovers interpretable governing equations from time-series data by sparse regression over a library of candidate functions. As data sets, candidate libraries and the number of repeated fits grow, the cost of SINDy becomes dominated by the evaluation of the library matrix and its least-squares problems. We present an open-source Python implementation of SINDy in which a single set of loop-based kernels is just-in-time compiled with Numba and executed on a shared-memory multicore node by four interchangeable back ends: Numba prange, OpenMP directives through PyOMP, Python threads that release the global interpreter lock, and multiprocessing with shared memory. The normal equations are accumulated in one cache-blocked pass over the data, with an automatic fallback to a tall-skinny QR reduction for ill-conditioned problems. Two decompositions are studied on the Lorenz and Rössler systems: an ensemble (task-parallel) workload of 960 independent identifications and a library (data-parallel) workload of one trajectory with $2\times10^7$ samples and a fifth-order library of 56 candidate terms. All back ends recover the exact sparsity pattern in every identification, with coefficient errors identical to PySINDy. On a dual-socket Intel Xeon E5-2650 v4 node of the University of Houston-Downtown HPC cluster (24 cores, 48 hardware threads), the compiled serial code is 4.3-5.6x faster than PySINDy, the parallel back ends reach speed-ups of 14.8-20.3 on 24 cores (parallel efficiency 0.61-0.84), and the overall time to solution is reduced by up to 117x with respect to PySINDy. Code and data: https://github.com/guillenrondonp/parallel-sindy</p></details> |

These weights represent a soft allocation of each sample-variable pair over shared temporal dependency structures, enabling the model to generate adaptive temporal dependency matrices under shared structural constraints, rather than fitting incidental interactions in a fully unconstrained manner. | **[Which Deep Learner? Specifically, the fitted SDEs identify regions of the state space where stochastic variability is greater, allowing a broader range of plausible subsequent changes. In addition, we show how the SDEs can be used to identify statistically atypical transitions in individual trajectories, indicative of exogenous perturbations impacting the associated society. The residual contrast between symmetric windows around the transition is compared with matched reference contrasts calculated within the contributing segments. The S-score is the standardized departure of the seam contrast from this reference. An S-score near zero indicates that the seam is consistent with ordinary temporal variability. Instead, these models exhibit low output alignment, resulting in fluctuation in prediction outputs for the same timestamps, undermining the model's reliability. | **[When Prediction Shapes the Future: A BlueSky Agenda for Reflexive Time Series Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.02511v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Time-series models are increasingly deployed in operational systems (e.g., inventory management and clinical care) where predictions trigger interventions that alter future observations, producing records we term AI-shaped trajectories. This sequence-prediction coupling deepens as such systems evolve into agents that autonomously predict, act, and remember across long horizons. Such AI-shaped trajectories support immediate performance monitoring, but turn ambiguous once reused as passive data. An algorithm may then misread a prediction-prevented stockout as naturally stable demand, or an averted medical crisis as a false alarm. This ambiguity then propagates into everything built on the record, from retraining and agent memory to downstream predictive tasks. As a result, systems may internalize these intervention effects as natural phenomena, drifting further from the truth at every closed-loop cycle. In this Blue Sky paper, we advocate an agenda for reflexive time-series analysis that shifts the analytical object from the intervention-agnostic sequence to the prediction-action-observation loop. The agenda requires closed-loop provenance to track the operational context (e.g., predictions, policies, and actions), sequence identification to estimate how those actions affect future values under explicit causal assumptions, and closed-loop evaluation to separate intrinsic process prediction from policy-driven effects. This foundation is essential for integrating autonomous agents into such loops at scale, enabling the joint learning of intervention effects and intrinsic dynamics for robust temporal representations.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to the ICDM 2026 BlueSky Track</p></details> |

The proposed framework also generates well-calibrated probabilistic forecasts within the range of 89.5% to 96.5% empirical coverage rate, has zero catastrophic forgetting (BWT = 0.0000) for all sequential learning tasks, and consistently shows positive forward transfer. Ablation and statistical significance analyses also validate the performance of adaptive quantum feature learning and continual adaptation under heterogeneous market conditions, regime-wise.</p></details> | 20 Pages, 8 Figures |
| **[Multimodal BVP and Respiration for Automated Pain Recognition with Hankel-FCN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01978v1)** | 2026-11-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated pain recognition is a fundamental challenge for the advancement of clinical care and empathetic human-computer interaction, due to the subjective and dynamic nature of pain. To explicitly model their temporal dynamics, the signals are transformed into structured trajectory representations using Hankel matrix embeddings, enabling the learning of dynamical patterns beyond conventional time-series representations. These embeddings are processed by modality-specific Fully Convolutional Networks, and their outputs are integrated through a late fusion strategy for three-class classification (No Pain, Low Pain, High Pain). Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method achieves an accuracy of 67.09% and an F1-score of 66.41%, providing competitive performance compared to existing approaches, while relying on no handcrafted feature engineering. Multimodal fusion significantly outperformed either modality alone, and the Hankel embedding showed a trend towards further improvement. Further validation is required to establish generalisability to broader populations and clinical settings.</p></details> |  |

Once trained, the model performs prediction for new admissions in a single forward pass, requiring neither retraining nor parameter adaptation on target datasets. However, real-world systems, from shifting financial cycles to elastic biological rhythms, frequently exhibit "time-warped" dynamics where the effective flow of time decouples from the sampling index. In this work, we first formalize this misalignment and prove that rotary position embedding (RoPE) is mathematically incapable of representing non-affine temporal warping. To address this, we propose Symplectic Positional Embeddings (SyPE), a learnable encoding framework derived from Hamiltonian mechanics. SyPE strictly generalizes RoPE by extending the rotation group $\mathrm{SO}(2)$ to the symplectic group $\mathrm{Sp}(2,\mathbb{R})$, modulated by a novel input-dependent adaptive warp module. By allowing the attention mechanism to adaptively dilate or contract temporal coordinates end-to-end, our approach captures locally varying periodicities without requiring pre-defined warping functions. We implement this mechanism in StretchTime, a multivariate forecasting architecture that achieves state-of-the-art performance on standard benchmarks, demonstrating superior robustness on datasets exhibiting non-stationary temporal dynamics.</p></details> | <details><summary>Publi...</summary><p>Published at ICML 2026. | **[When Relationships Break: Interpreting Network Traffic Anomalies via Dependency Violations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26831v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current research on security monitoring is increasingly focusing on machine-learning-based approaches, but caveats remain. In addition to huge computational overhead, one concern is the lack of insights into ``why" alerts are raised. Existing interpretability approaches rely on feature attribution methods that ignore dependencies among features or on causal modeling that requires extensive domain knowledge or computational resources. This work proposes XION, a method for modeling relationships among network-flow features based on benign traffic only. During detection, anomalies are identified through violations of expected feature dependencies. Further, XION supports post-alert analysis by identifying which feature relationships break, when they break along the attack timeline, and how dependency violations evolve relatively to other identified violations. XION is evaluated on standard IDS datasets and compared against an Isolation Forest (IF) baseline across multiple attack scenarios, including both volumetric and stealthier attacks. Results show that XION matches or exceeds IF recall in all evaluated scenarios, while requiring up to 7$\times$ less inference time. At the post-alert stage, the dependency violation analysis reveals temporal and structural patterns consistent with known attack behaviors, and is further evaluated against SHAP-based feature attribution of IF alerts. Together, these findings confirm that attacks indeed disrupt feature dependencies learned from benign traffic, and that these disruptions provide additional information for understanding an alert.</p></details> |  |
| **[Traffic-VL: Reinforced Vision-Language Models for Generalizable End-to-End Traffic Signal Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06353v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing traffic signal control methods typically rely on structured traffic states produced by dedicated perception pipelines. Emerging visual approaches often use idealized simulator images and convert them into intermediate representations before control, limiting their ability to directly exploit roadside visual observations. To bridge both the visual-domain and perception-to-decision gaps, we introduce SynTra-V and Traffic-VL. SynTra-V converts physics-based traffic simulations into photorealistic multi-view surveillance imagery across diverse road topologies and sensing conditions, and automatic semantic auditing checks that control-critical geometry, vehicle movements, and lane semantics match the simulator state. Traffic-VL is an eight-billion-parameter vision-language model that directly maps synchronized multi-view images and natural-language phase descriptions to signal decisions without intermediate structured states. Building on Traffic-R1's two-stage reinforced reasoning framework, Traffic-VL adapts its vision encoder via offline reinforcement learning on SynTra-V and freezes it during online visual stepwise trajectory policy optimization in closed-loop simulation. Experiments show that Traffic-VL matches structured-state Traffic-R1 while outperforming image-based controllers and general-purpose vision-language models, including Gemini 3 Pro. Evaluations on unseen topologies, degraded sensing conditions, real surveillance footage, and a three-week deployment across ten intersections further demonstrate robust visual generalization and effective constrained online control.</p></details> |  |
| **[The Split Protocol Decides the Result: Re-Evaluating Encrypted Traffic Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06290v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Encrypted traffic classifiers are routinely reported at 95-99% accuracy. Nothing changes but the partition (gap 0.426 over eight seeds, p = 0.008 after Holm-Bonferroni correction). The inflation is not a property of random splitting as such. On a month of backbone QUIC traffic, we evaluate five split protocols at eight seeds each: random, temporal and client-disjoint splitting are statistically indistinguishable from session-disjoint, while server-disjoint splitting costs 0.192 macro-F1 (d = 17.0, p = 0.008). Which axis matters depends on how the corpus was collected, in a way invisible from its label space. We give a diagnostic that identifies the right axis before any model is trained: single-identifier probes. A decision tree on the capture identifier alone reaches 0.983 under a random split and exactly 0.000 under session-disjoint splitting. Five of our own pre-registered architectural claims fail: distance-based rejection of unknown applications beats softmax thresholding, but ablating every metric objective makes rejection better, because class separation correlates at r = -0.88 with rejection quality. Experimental results demonstrate more than 99% transmission savings and an average trajectory reconstruction error of only 0.12 m, confirming the proposed framework as an effective, scalable, and low-latency solution for AI-enabled urban traffic digital twin applications.</p></details> | <details><summary>6 pag...</summary><p>6 pages, 4 figures, accepted to IEEE Global Communications (Globecom) Conference, 2026</p></details> |
| **[Maneuver Coordination Using V2I to Improve Traffic Efficiency at Intersections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13394v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Traffic efficiency and air pollutants are major issues in urban transport systems, as they are critical points for traffic flows and people living in urban areas, respectively. ## Graph Neural Networks
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[GraphIP-Bench: How Hard Is It to Steal a Graph Neural Network, and Can We Stop It?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12827v3)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph neural networks (GNNs) deployed as cloud services can be *stolen* through *model-extraction attacks*, which train a surrogate from query responses to reproduce the target's behaviour, and a growing line of ownership defenses tries to prevent or trace such theft. The title of this paper asks two questions: *how hard is it to steal a GNN?*, and *can we stop it?* Prior work cannot answer either, because experiments use inconsistent datasets, threat models, and metrics. We introduce *GraphIP-Bench*, a unified benchmark which evaluates both sides under a single black-box protocol. It integrates twelve extraction attacks, twelve defenses spanning watermarking, output-perturbation, and query-pattern-detection families, ten public graphs covering homophilic, heterophilic, and large-scale regimes, three GNN backbones, and three graph-learning tasks, and it reports fidelity, task utility, ownership verification, and computational cost on shared splits, queries, and budgets. We further add a joint attack-and-defense track which runs every attack on every defended target and measures watermark verification on the resulting surrogate, which exposes the protection that a defense retains after extraction. The empirical picture is short: stealing a GNN is easy at medium query budgets and most defenses do not change this; several watermarks verify reliably on the protected model but lose most of their verification signal on the extracted surrogate, which exposes a gap that single-model evaluations miss; and heterophilic graphs are systematically harder to steal, while a cross-architecture mismatch between target and surrogate reduces but does not prevent extraction. We release *GraphIP-Bench* with reproducible scripts and configurations, and integrate the attacks and defenses into the PyGIP library.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. Code: https://github.com/LabRAI/GraphIP-Bench</p></details> |
| **[Message-passing graph persistence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06801v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Message-passing graph neural networks (GNNs) are limited in expressive power by the one-dimensional Weisfeiler-Leman test. Persistent homology (PH) provides topological descriptors that can complement GNN representations, but existing approaches typically compute persistence separately from the representation learning process. In this paper, we introduce Message-Passing Graph Persistence (MGP), a family of topological descriptors that incorporates message passing into graph filtrations. At each filtration step, node embeddings are updated using the current subgraph and associated with persistence events. These embeddings also determine the representatives of connected components during persistence computation. We prove that MGP is invariant under graph isomorphism and that it is strictly more expressive than the PH-based descriptors it extends. We evaluate MGP on an expressivity benchmark and on several graph classification and regression datasets. Experiments show that MGP outperforms other PH-based descriptors on most of these datasets.</p></details> |  |
| **[Homomorphism Indistinguishability Beyond Graphs: Relational Weisfeiler--Leman and Hypertree Width](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07934v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Weisfeiler--Leman (WL) algorithm is one of the most influential heuristics for the graph isomorphism problem. The expressive power of WL has been extensively studied in the contexts of descriptive complexity, logics, graph neural networks, and the theory of homomorphism indistinguishabily. Notably, two graphs are indistinguishable by the $k$-dimensional WL algorithm if and only if they are indistinguishable by homomorphism-counts from graphs of treewidth at most $k$. An intrinsic question is to find a natural version of the WL algorithm for relational structures of higher arity admitting an equivalent characterisation via homomorphism indistinguishability along bounded generalised hypertree width (GHW). Scheidt and Schweikardt solved this for $k=1$ by defining the RCR algorithm and showing indistinguishability from $α$-acyclic structures. In this work, we resolve this for all $k\ge1$: we develop a $k$-dimensional version of RCR ($k$-RCR for short) and show that two structures $\mathcal A$ and $\mathcal B$ are indistinguishable by $k$-RCR if and only if they have the same number of homomorphisms from all structures $\mathcal C$ of generalised hypertreewidth at most $k$. Furthermore, we show that on a structure $\mathcal A$ over a fixed signature, with $\mathsf{ghw}(\mathcal{A})\leq k$, $k$-RCR runs in $O(\|\mathcal A\|^k\cdot\log(\|\mathcal A\|))$, where $\|\mathcal A\|$ is the number of all tuples that appear in the relations of $\mathcal A$.</p></details> |  |
| **[From Lab to Reality: A Practical Evaluation of Deep Learning Models and LLMs for Vulnerability Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.10485v4)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vulnerability detection methods based on deep learning (DL) have shown strong performance on benchmark datasets, yet their real-world effectiveness remains underexplored. Each model is trained independently on each dataset, and the graph-based and CodeBERT representations adopted by these models are analyzed using t-SNE and centroid distance to examine vulnerability-related patterns. Our experiments reveal that current representation methods struggle to distinguish vulnerable from non-vulnerable code and that trained models generalize poorly across datasets with differing distributions and characteristics. When evaluated on VentiVul, performance drops sharply, with most models failing to detect vulnerabilities reliably or distinguish vulnerable functions from their patched counterparts. These results expose a persistent gap between academic benchmarks and real-world deployment, emphasizing the value of our deployment-oriented evaluation framework and the need for more robust code representations, higher-quality datasets, and evaluation methods that account for vulnerability-fixing changes.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted for publication in Journal of Systems and Software (JSS)</p></details> |

Crucially, HDF encodes structure more efficiently: at low dimensionalities, its distances correlate with graph edit distance more strongly than Morgan fingerprints', yielding effective similarity metrics and competitive nearest-neighbor regression at just 64 components. Supplementary information included as an ancillary file (anc/supplementary.pdf). Revised version after peer review</p></details> |
| **[Learning Pseudo-Riemannian Manifolds for Heterophilic Graphs via Graph Signature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06265v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Several widely used families of Graph Neural Networks carry a positive-definite geometric prior. On heterophilic graphs, where edges often signify dissimilarity or structural repulsion, that prior is a mismatch: no positive-definite form can assign a negative squared distance to a direction the labels separate. To measure the mismatch, we introduce the Graph Signature Index ($s_{\mathcal{G}}$), the inertia of a class-compatibility matrix built from label topology alone, and we prove that no value of the edge or node homophily index in $(0,1)$ rules out a negative eigenvalue. We then propose the Pseudo-Riemannian Attention Network (PRAT), whose attention logit is a signed squared pseudo-distance under a learnable diagonal metric initialized from $s_{\mathcal{G}}$. The design is diagnose-then-learn: the signature supplies a sign structure that unconstrained training keeps outside near-zero directions, and the projection learns the rest of the metric around it. | **[What Does It Mean to Remove an Edge? Structural deletion removes the edge from the graph and recomputes quantities such as degree normalization and attention, whereas message suppression leaves this structural context intact while blocking the message carried by the edge. The two interventions coincide for additive message passing with edge-set-independent coefficients, but can diverge when aggregation depends on the presence of zero messages or when removing an edge changes the coefficients applied to surviving messages. We then construct intervention-specific edge attributions and evaluate each resulting ranking under both interventions, testing whether their relative fidelity changes with the evaluation intervention. Across nine datasets and a panel of GIN, GCN, and GATv2 models at depths 2--5, all nine GIN controls satisfy the equivalence predicted by the theory. On the target logit, the relative fidelity of the two rankings depends on the evaluation intervention in 10 of 11 reportable GCN and GATv2 cells, and in nine of them each ranking has the larger deletion area under the intervention it was built with. Evaluation protocols should explicitly report the intervention, output functional, target, and perturbation unit, and test alternative intervention semantics when the scientific question does not uniquely determine one.</p></details> | <details><summary>15 pa...</summary><p>15 pages, 2 figures. | **[Sheaf-MPNN: A Library for Sheaf Neural Networks as Message Passing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04670v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Standard Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) implicitly assume that neighboring nodes share the same feature space, which contributes to oversmoothing and poor performance on heterophilic graphs. To provide a principled explanation of the expected performance improvement, we contrast sheaf diffusion to graph attention mechanisms, highlighting that DNSD replaces scalar attention scores with matrix-valued edge functions and normalizes node representations rather than attention scores. We demonstrate empirically that DNSD effectively utilizes deep aggregation in graph tasks, outperforming GNN and NSD baselines with up to 30pp accuracy on synthetic long-range datasets, and consistently outperforming them on real-world benchmarks. | **[How Much Graph Is Too Much? | **[Label Ensemble Perspective for Reduced-Complexity Graph Node Classification under Label Noise](https://arxiv.org/abs/2411.11020v2)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) have been widely employed for semi-supervised node classification tasks on graphs. Because all of these are computed from the same ingredients, what changes is not the amount of information but its form. The CFL conditioning proves most effective when the edge information already exposes the transport interaction, reducing final-step error by 10.4-14.4%, and the improvement widens as the step size grows larger. Notably, this improvement arises not from smearing the solution but from suppressing oscillations near shocks. These results point to a specific design principle that CFL conditioning is most effective when the edge representation already separates the direction of interaction from spatial scale, so that the CFL value has a well-defined quantity to modulate.</p></details> |  |

This feature-reweighting mechanism reduces reliance on spurious correlations without erasing information, offering a label-free, continuous relaxation of last-layer reweighting methods. ## Contrastive Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[MMAP: Multimodal Missing-Aware Pretraining for Longitudinal Alzheimer's Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26617v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clinical decision making heavily relies on predicting the disease progression trajectory by seeking to understand patient's health status which is characterised by multimodal medical data. AI holds great potential for learning useful representations from multimodal medical data to predict disease progression and aid clinical decision making. However, development of predictive AI models is constrained by missing modalities and incomplete tabular data frequently occurring in medical datasets. In addition, disease labels alone may only provide limited supervisory signals for learning representations from high-dimensional multimodal data. Here, we present MMAP, a novel Multimodal Missing-aware Alignment Pretraining method for learning image-tabular representations from incomplete data. An image encoder is pretrained with efficient sigmoid contrastive learning combined with generative reconstruction. A tabular encoder is built upon a tabular foundation model. A missing token generator enables the two encoders to take incomplete data as input, enabling the model to be robust against missing modalities, either with missing images or missing tabular data. We evaluate the clinical usefulness of the learnt multimodal representations on two challenging longitudinal clinical tasks for Alzheimer's disease: predicting disease stage conversion and predicting amyloid status. The proposed method outperforms strong multimodal and unimodal baselines.</p></details> | <details><summary>To be...</summary><p>To be published in the proceedings of the 2026 MICCAI Workshop on Multimodal Learning with Medical Tabular Data</p></details> |
| **[PatchCon: Patch-aware Weighted Supervised Contrastive Learning for Vulnerability Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06583v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Automated vulnerability detection is critical for securing modern software systems, yet existing learning-based approaches often treat functions as independent samples and underuse the supervision provided by vulnerability-fixing patches. In this paper, we propose PatchCon, a CodeBERT-based patch-aware contrastive learning model for function-level vulnerability detection. PatchCon jointly optimizes vulnerability classification and supervised contrastive learning, and treats vulnerable-patched function pairs as hard negatives with larger contrastive weights to capture subtle security-relevant differences introduced by fixes. To evaluate patch-level discrimination, we use two pairwise metrics that measure whether a model can correctly distinguish vulnerable functions from their patched counterparts. We evaluate PatchCon on PairVul-Linux through extensive experiments, including state-of-the-art comparisons, ablation studies, hyperparameter sensitivity analysis, and statistical significance testing. We also conduct fine-grained analyses by Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE) type, function length, and patch size, and further evaluate PatchCon on Linux kernel vulnerabilities disclosed after the training data cutoff. On PairVul-Linux, PatchCon outperforms representative pretrained code models and contrastive learning baselines on both classification and pairwise metrics, improves pairwise accuracy over the strongest reproduced baseline from 0.2060 to 0.3264, and achieves pairwise accuracy comparable to that of a GPT-4o-based large language model (LLM) method at a substantially lower cost. Among the reproduced models, PatchCon also achieves the highest pairwise accuracy on test pairs from Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVE) entries unseen during training and on newly disclosed vulnerabilities.</p></details> |  |
| **[$A^3$-VLA: Automatic Perception-Guided Attention Alignment for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06447v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language-Action Models (VLAs) enable robots to perform manipulation tasks by leveraging Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to process visual observations and language instructions. However, the action prediction objective itself lacks fine-grained task-specific perceptual supervision, making it prone to relying on task-irrelevant visual shortcuts, leading attention away from target objects and key interaction areas, thereby limiting its operational performance and generalization ability. To address this, we propose Automatic Perception-Guided Attention Alignment for Robot Manipulation ($A^3$-VLA), a framework that derives task-specific visual guidance from the manipulation process itself. Specifically, $A^3$-VLA first generates heuristic candidate regions for target objects and the robot from interaction-induced visual changes, and refines them with a visual segmentation model offline. These regions serve as explicit perceptual guidance through two complementary objectives: an attention allocation loss that encourages sufficient focus on task-critical regions when language or robot state tokens query the visual input, and a contrastive learning loss that aligns the representations of candidate target regions with language instructions while separating them from task-irrelevant regions. Extensive experiments on simulated and real-world manipulation tasks demonstrate that $A^3$-VLA consistently improves multiple VLA backbones, with an average success rate gain of 6.5% - 25.2% across benchmarks.</p></details> |  |
| **[CAPE-JEPA - Cross-modal Adversarial Probabilistic Embedding JEPA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06247v1)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cross-modal remote sensing image retrieval (RS-CMIR) requires representations that are simultaneously modality-invariant and semantically discriminative, despite the profound physical gap between heterogeneous sensors such as Synthetic Aperture Radar (SAR) and multispectral imagery. We propose CAPE-JEPA, a probabilistic cross-modal Joint Embedding Predictive Architecture with three contributions: (i) a cross-attentional Gaussian predictor outputting per-patch parameters (mu, sigma^2) conditioned on opposite-modality context, trained via negative log-likelihood to model inherent cross-sensor prediction uncertainty; (ii) adversarial distributional regularization combining unimodal isotropic Gaussian regularization with a novel cross-modal adversarial variant that aligns modality distributions via worst-case Epps-Pulley characteristic function tests without explicit sample pairing; and (iii) in-training contrastive learning with attention-pooled heads for scene-level discriminative grounding. The bank stores ground-truth-labeled features only where the student prediction agrees with the label. This simplifies earlier labeled-pixel banks by removing confidence thresholds and learned ranking. On Pascal VOC with 183 labels, PixCon improves all three paired seeds by 0.20, 0.64 and 1.83 mIoU points, raising the mean from 87.01 to 87.90. A predicted-label contrastive control reaches nearly the same Pascal accuracy, so these results support the added branch rather than an accuracy benefit from its correctness filter. An exact gradient decomposition distinguishes mislabeled-positive counts from their similarity-weighted influence. Separate teacher diagnostics find retained-pixel error rates of 1.8% on Pascal and 10.6% on ADE20K. These diagnostics do not measure the training bank or predict final accuracy. PixCon adds 15.3-19.7% iteration time in the measured runs and no inference computation. We further validate the method through additional analyses of robustness to stronger augmentations, label efficiency under limited fine-tuning labels, and computational trade-offs. | **[WiMoT: Replay-Free Continual Wi-Fi Sensing via Moment Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32660v3)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Continual Wi-Fi sensing must learn new rooms or users (plasticity) while keeping earlier ones (stability), yet sensing nodes often cannot retain channel state information (CSI) for privacy and storage reasons. On 3DO and Widar3.0 it is 3.77 and 13.11 pp ahead of the strongest of 21 baselines, which uses replay; on WiMANS it matches the strongest replay baseline in accuracy while reducing forgetting from 2.78 to 0.00 pp. Rank-32 covariance factors shrink the moment memory to 6.4% at a cost of at most 0.49 pp.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 6 figures, 10 tables; supplementary material: 13 pages. This work has been submitted to the IEEE for possible publication. Copyright may be transferred without notice, after which this version may no longer be accessible. Code: https://github.com/Newsteinwell/WiMoT</p></details> |
| **[ProAlign: Progressive Positional and Prototype-Guided Alignment for Aerial-Ground Person Re-Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04980v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Aerial-Ground Person Re-Identification (AG-ReID) aims to match pedestrian images captured by unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) and ground-based surveillance cameras. | **[PAT: A Personality-Driven Augmentation and Transfer Learning Framework for Depression Detection on Social Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15044v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Depression is a prevalent mental disorder that affects millions worldwide, with early detection crucial for effective intervention. Extensive experiments demon?strate that PAT significantly outperforms exist?ing methods. Furthermore, PAT delivers multi?granular interpretability by tracing post-level mental-state trajectories and highlighting key symptoms, thereby offering reliable diagnostic evidence for clinical practice.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning to Rank Explanations for Recommendation with Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04775v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recommender systems are ubiquitous on the Web, making understandable explanations essential for users. Although large language models (LLMs) increasingly generate natural-language justifications, evaluating their factual grounding and personalized relevance remains challenging. Statement-level ranking offers an alternative by selecting concise, review-derived explanations, enabling fine-grained and reproducible evaluation using established ranking metrics. However, existing statement ranking methods largely rely on collaborative representations, while semantic enhancements often fail to outperform simple user-history popularity baselines. Moreover, directly adapting language models for personalized statement ranking remains largely unexplored. In this work, we introduce LMStaR (Language Models for Statement Ranking), a fully text-based framework that formulates personalized explanation ranking as dense retrieval. A query encoder represents the user's interaction history and target-item metadata, while a statement encoder maps candidate explanations into a shared embedding space. Both encoders are jointly adapted through multi-positive contrastive learning to learn personalized relevance. Experiments on Amazon Beauty, Amazon Toys, and Yelp show that LMStaR achieves the highest NDCG@1 across all three domains, outperforming CLER by 1.06--8.25 points (+11.8--65.9%). It also leads top-five ranking on both Amazon datasets, although CLER remains stronger on Yelp. These findings highlight the potential of task-adapted language models for personalized explanation ranking.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages, 5 figures, 7 tables</p></details> |
| **[Uncertainty-Aware Prototype Learning for Open-Set Test-Time Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04689v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Open-set test-time adaptation (TTA) aims to adapt source-trained models to unlabeled test streams where covariate-shifted in-distribution (csID) samples and covariate-shifted out-of-distribution (csOOD) samples may coexist. Consequently, csOOD samples may be mistakenly included in confidence-enhancing adaptation, producing harmful gradients that degrade known-class performance. To address this, we propose Uncertainty-Aware Prototype Learning for Open-Set Test-Time Adaptation (UAPA), a plug-and-play method for reliable open-set adaptation. Specifically, we first introduce Uncertainty-Driven ID/OOD Separation, which combines a classifier-prototype score with a mini-batch-normalized evidence-derived uncertainty score to distinguish csID from csOOD samples. We then propose a Dynamic Prototype Refinement strategy that periodically refines class-specific target prototypes using buffered csID features to progressively follow the estimated class directions. Lastly, we introduce Contrastive Prototype Entropy Adaptation, which applies entropy minimization to csID samples and entropy maximization to csOOD samples, while prototype-level contrastive learning aligns csID features with their corresponding target prototypes and reduces the maximum similarity between csOOD features and known-class prototypes. | **[Polyp Matching Using Deep Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04624v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A key challenge in colon capsule endoscopy (CCE) analysis is accurately identifying whether multiple images depict the same polyp, which is critical to avoid over-counting and unnecessary clinical interventions. The model was trained on a dataset from the Danish CareForColon2015 trial. The proposed model employs ResNet variants (ResNet18, ResNet34, ResNet50), DenseNet121 and EfficientNet B0/B3 as backbones and utilizes contrastive learning with cosine vector distance metric for classification. Experimental results demonstrate high classification accuracies of up to 92% and AUC values close to 0.98. | **[Polarimetric Scattering-Structure Contrastive Learning for Few-Shot HRRP Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04490v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Few-shot high-resolution range profile (HRRP) recognition is challenged by strong aspect-dependent variation and sparse novel-class support.
